Theorem 5.19 (Exact fiber-chart invariance). Let Ψ1 (w, z1 ) and Ψ2 (w, z2 ) be two exact C 1 fiber charts

                      Z                                   Z

Consequently the normalized conditional expectation, its connected logarithm, and every background

of the chosen exact fiber chart.


                                                        48
Proof. The first identity is the ordinary change-of-variables theorem on the fiber. Division by the same
fiber mass gives equality of normalized conditional expectations. Connected logarithms are functions
of those expectations. Differentiation is permitted on any common dominated marked domain. The
determinant statement follows because the two Jacobians are related by the determinant of the transition
map, which cancels under the change of variables. It must not be inserted a second time.

5.5    Finite triangular source transport
Lemma 5.21 (Finite graded curvature-jet quotient). Fix an engineering cutoff D. Modulo vacuum
constants, total lattice differences, gauge and Bianchi identities, equation-of-motion/redundant directions,
and the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills coordinate, the local gauge-invariant curvature jets of
dimension at most D form a finite graded space
                                                      M
                                               J≤D =       J∆ .
                                                         ∆≤D

The same remains true after adjoining any fixed finite collection of retained, source, displacement, profile,
stress, rotation-defect, and one/two-root marks.
Proof. After a boundary-rooted tree gauge, one enlarged block cell contains a finite alphabet of Lie-algebra
variables and difference labels. Jets of bounded engineering dimension form a finite polynomial span.
Every listed quotient relation is linear on that span, and adjoining finitely many marks gives a finite
direct sum.
Lemma 5.22 (No reflection-even scalar of engineering dimension five). In four-dimensional pure fixed-G
lattice gauge theory, the signed-hypercubic and reflection-even gauge-invariant scalar jet quotient has no
engineering-dimension-five component.
Proof. A nonvacuum dimension-five scalar would have to contain two curvature factors and one covariant
difference, hence five coordinate indices before contraction. The invariant tensors of the signed hypercubic
group are generated by even-rank Kronecker contractions and the rank-four alternating tensor. They
cannot contract an odd total of five indices to a scalar without a preferred vector. Total differences and
Bianchi descendants are already removed in the quotient. Thus the reflection-even scalar sector jumps
from the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills density to dimension at least six.

1     The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary
1.1    Problem and result
The constructive Yang–Mills problem asks for a nontrivial quantum gauge field theory on R4 satisfying
the Osterwalder–Schrader and Wightman requirements and possessing a strictly positive mass gap [1].
The physical scale, thermodynamic limit, zero lattice spacing, Euclidean covariance, local spectral content,
and non-abelian field geometry must be controlled in one compatible construction.
   Fix throughout a compact connected simple Lie group G, a faithful unitary representation ρ : G → U (Vρ )
of dimension dρ , and a positive normalization of the invariant quadratic form for which the adjoint color
factor is denoted CA (G). The Wilson plaquette function is
                                                        1
                                                                    
                                   wG,ρ (U ) = cρ 1 −     Re Tr ρ(U ) ,
                                                       dρ
where cρ > 0 is chosen once so that the Hessian at the identity is the quadratic form used by the
background-field coupling coordinate u = g 2 . This choice is a finite linear normalization of the bare
Wilson coupling and is part of the fixed-group input register.
Theorem 1.1 (Four-dimensional Yang–Mills construction for fixed compact simple G). For every fixed
pair (G, ρ) as above, there is a directed sequence of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures, with
microscopic spacing tending to zero and periodic volume tending to infinity, whose local gauge-invariant
curvature-jet Schwinger functions converge to a nontrivial Euclidean field theory on R4 .
   The limiting Schwinger family is tempered, permutation symmetric, reflection positive, translation
continuous, exponentially clustering, and O(4) covariant. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction yields a
positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with a unique vacuum and a number m⋆ (G) > 0 such that
                                              H Ω⊥ ≥ m⋆ (G).
On the centered invariant weak trajectory, the exact scale-two coupling map is
                                            11 CA (G) log 2
                           u(2L) = u(L) +                   u(L)2 + OG (u(L)3 ),
                                                 24π 2
with uniform volume and cutoff control in the permanent weak regime. The reconstructed theory contains
a nonzero reflected local curvature vector and positive spectral mass in a finite interval [m⋆ (G), M⋆ (G)].
1.2    Fixed-group conventions and finite register
Write g = Lie(G) and dG = dim G. All Haar measures are normalized. The finite-volume approximants
use the ordinary periodic link space GE(Λ) with periodic gauge transformations; no twisted boundary
condition or nontrivial finite-volume bundle sector is summed. This is sufficient for the claimed local
theory on the contractible space R4 . The bi-invariant metric, exponential coordinates, Laplacian ∆G ,
Casimir eigenvalues, and operator norms are those determined by the declared quadratic form. In
curvature formulas, Tr(XY ) denotes this normalized invariant bilinear pairing; raw representation traces
are written Tr ρ(·). Let                     
                                              dG                    dG
                                      sG :=       + 1,      2sG >       .
                                               4                      2
The fixed-group constant register CG consists of the finite quantities used below: the injectivity and
convexity radii, a positive local Karcher radius, finite holomorphic BCH and logarithm bounds through
order 2sG + 3, the faithful-action Hessian and complement charge, the Peter–Weyl constant, the finite
ordinary-periodic toron/seam template register, the adjoint color factor, and the finite invariant-jet
dimensions through the retained degree. Every statement called uniform is uniform in cutoff, volume,
directed depth, periodic wrapping, admitted exterior histories, and the finite reflected template family,
but its constants may depend on CG .
Proposition 1.2 (Reflection-positive Wilson input). For every finite periodic lattice Λ and u > 0, in the
ordinary periodic link sector,

                                     cG ∥X∥2 ≤ wG,ρ (eX ) ≤ CG ∥X∥2 .

Proof. The normalized character satisfies Re Tr ρ(U ) ≤ dρ , with equality only when ρ(U ) = I; faithfulness
gives U = e. The Hessian statement follows from the chosen normalization and compactness. Up to a field-
independent constant, each plaquette factor is exp{t(χρ +χρ )} with t > 0. Its power series is a nonnegative
linear combination of characters of tensor products of ρ and ρ̄, and their irreducible decompositions
have nonnegative integer multiplicities. The standard character-expansion reflection-positivity argument
therefore applies.

1.3     Proof architecture
The proof has four stages. First, the exact Karcher block is placed in a complete full-alias normal chart
and combined with a charged chart-complement partition, permanent analytic domain, marked connected
logarithm, Wick-stable jet block, and centered invariant graph. The folded spacetime symbol tensors
with Ig , while all nonlinear estimates are admitted by the finite register CG .
   Second, exact background-scheme conjugacy identifies the universal marginal coefficient      and a directed
schedule reaches the compact matching scale. The higher product Sobolev operator p (1 − ∆p )sG replaces
                                                                                       Q
the rank-one order and supplies the fixed-G Fourier–Wiener rows.
   Third, complete Haar and optical variables are kept in one partition function. A mixed replica–
BKAR forest makes every root-to-root path pay separated Haar contraction, optical covariance decay,
or pre-existing support charge. Ordinary-periodic toron backgrounds, seams, reflections, and wrapping
certificates enter only through the finite template theorem of Appendix D.
   Finally, a least-label source forest transports the complete invariant-jet system. The degree-four block
is group-independent because a compact simple Lie algebra has a unique invariant symmetric bilinear
form; possible cubic color tensors first enter the finite dimension-six block. Ward identities, rotation-defect
estimates, reflection-positive recombination, and Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction give covariance,
the unique vacuum, the gap, and noncollapse.

Only the theorem-level inputs in Table 1 are imported. The fixed-group geometry, action charge, higher
Sobolev landing, periodic-toron template theorem, invariant-jet admission, matching constants, and
noncollapse source are internal.

1.5     Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction
The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful
independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is
stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives
a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which
tensor with Ig , and which require new group data.
1.6     Scope
The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact
connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit,
control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum
limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link
group. The construction uses only the ordi

Proof. On the inner weak chart the uniform marked C 3 theorem gives, for each of hj , Σj , and Fj , a
Taylor expansion with a remainder whose kth scalar derivative is bounded by Ck u3−k , 0 ≤ k ≤ 2. The
exactly recombined charged complement contributes the displayed C 3 -flat row. Tree level and the unit
tangent are volume independent. Lemma 5.4 compares the remaining quadratic coefficients of hj and
Σj with their thermodynamic coefficients by CδG (Mj ); permanently localized histories contribute the
exponential part already included in (11).
   For the reduced map, expand the exact finite-volume intertwining identity

                                             hj+1 ◦ Fj = Σj ◦ hj

through second order. If the quadratic coefficients of hj , hj+1 , and Σj are aj , aj+1 , and bj , respectively,
then the quadratic coefficient of Fj is bj + aj − aj+1 . Since Mj+1 = Mj /2 and δG (Mj /2) ≤ CG δG (Mj ) on
the admitted range, the same finite-size row holds for Fj . Taylor’s theorem and the flat charged row now
give (13). Under (14), the finite-size term and its first derivative are cubic and quadratic, respectively,
while the flat term is smaller than every power. Composition with the unit-tangent hj proves (16).
5.2.3 Measure-level intertwining
For each scheduled step let Bj∗ be the exact Karcher pushforward from scale aj to aj+1 , including
canonical rescaling and exact regular

=== coupling coordinate hit 3 at 427950 ===
                               k≥0

whose coefficients are C 2 on the relative interior of that cell. Every normalization, denominator, localiza-
tion, irregular and source-motion contact belongs to exactly one selected packet. No assertion that the
individual coefficients FM,k agree for two different stopping factorizations is needed or made.
Proof. At fixed finite volume and depth, the reporting-telescope and owner-map identities form a disjoint
finite or absolutely convergent inventory of complete normalized packets. Their canonical physical reported
supports therefore admit disjoint scalar dyadic selectors after packet completion. Inside one crossing
cell the stopping depth and exact factorization are fixed, so the graph-segment C 2 identity and the
finite marked forest permit termwise differentiation. Summing the selectors gives the unselected exact
functional, proving (190).
Definition A.6 (Finite directed continuation and crossing cells). Fix the physical weak scale r, its
               √
endpoint gr = ur , and an arbitrary finite further directed depth M . Choose one terminal coarse side

               u0 (g) = g 2 ,    ui+1 (g) = Fi (ui (g)),   gi (g) = ui (g),   0 ≤ i < M.
                                                                   p

Let
                                  NM (g) := min{0 ≤ i ≤ M : ui (g) ≥ ū},
with NM (g) = ∞ if the set is empty. Uniform outgoing transversality implies that, when NM (g) < ∞,

                               ui (g) < ū   (i < NM (g)),      uNM (g) (g) ∈ I⋆ .

The relative interiors of the sets on which NM (g) = N are called the crossing cells. On such a cell the
exact factorization consists of N weak conditionals followed by the printed finite macrobridge and joint
compact–optical logarithm. On the no-crossing cell it consists of all M weak conditionals followed by the
terminal weak logarithm on the fixed coarse torus.


                                                      96
   This is a choice of exact factorization, not a modification of the measure. The integer NM (g) is never
differentiated. Derivatives are taken inside one crossing cell, where the depth is fixed, and are extended
to cell boundaries using the original finite-volume C 2 normalized functional. At g = 0 one always uses
the no-crossing factorization and later sends M → ∞.
Definition A.7 (Canonical finite-volume off-graph coordinates). For the directed schedule in Defini-
tion A.6, put
                           εi := e−σMi ,     δi := δG (Mi ),    Mi = 2M −i Mout .
The first quantity records genuine wrapping histories in the interaction; the second records the scalar
massless finite-size comparison. After each complete connected output has been formed and assigned its
canonical reported hull, let Piwr select precisely the history certificates containing at least one periodic
wrapping occurrence at some scale not later than i. If Ri is the exact finite-volume centered interaction
and R⋆ (ui ) is the rooted-thermodynamic invariant graph, set

                      Ωi := Ri − R⋆ (ui ),       Wi := Piwr Ωi ,     Bi := (I − Piwr )Ωi .               (191)

Thus Ωi = Wi + Bi coefficientwise. Both Ri and R⋆ (ui ) are written in the same exact schedule-fixed
vacuum and tangent-normalized coupling coordinates. The coordinate intertwining and supportwise
                                    [2]
extraction identity therefore give EX Ωi,X = 0 for every reported support X; all three off-graph coordinates
lie in the actual centered stable fiber. The first coordinate is the canonical sum of histories carrying a
wrapping certificate. After the tangent-normalized scalar coordinate is extracted, the massless acoustic
sum–integral correction belongs to the nonautonomous scalar map Fi and is not an off-graph activity.
By the exact regular interaction comparison and label recombination, the only nonwrapping off-graph
difference is the slaved U4 charged/atlas complement; this is Bi and is exponentially flat in ui . No flat
charged coefficient is identified with a wrapping history.

Proof. Use the complete labelled connected-history representation before forgetting owners. In the
scheduled torus and in the rooted thermodynamic step, pair a history whenever its canonically reported
hull has an injective periodic lift and contains no charged atlas label. The local Karcher, Gaussian, coarea,
determinant, localization, extraction and collect templates are then literally the same, so paired histories
and their derivatives cancel coefficientwise. An unpaired regular history has a nontrivial periodic cycle;
its reported hull has length at least cMi . Weakening the permanent rooted support exponent once gives
Ce−σ0 Mi . An unpaired charged history belongs to the exactly recombined U4 row and is Cu−P e−c0 /u .
   For (193), insert H as one distinguished input atom before the connected logarithm. The permanent
one-step derivative envelope is a rooted absolute sum with that atom retained once. The same periodic-lift
pairing applies, and the long hull still pays e−σ0 Mi ; no derivative can shorten the already assigned reported
hull. Vacuum, two-jet and coupling extraction are finite-range linear maps and are paired in the same way.


                                                       97
Passing to the adapted norm is harmless: that norm is a fixed finite sum of rooted norms of powers of the
rooted-thermodynamic linear map, and each power preserves the preceding paired/unpaired classification.
   At R = R⋆ (u), the free term cancels because both maps are written in the same schedule-fixed vacuum
coordinates. The stable tangent uH1 is exponentially local but need not have finite support, so an
unpaired periodic image may already be of order εi u. This sharp row gives (192); no quadratic stable
cancellation is asserted. If the output is projected to the wrapping ideal, a charged history without a
prior periodic certificate must also acquire a new nontrivial periodic cycle. It therefore pays both the
charged flat row and εi+1 . The same observation applied to a nonwrapping distinguished input proves
(194) and (195). These are statements about the full centered interaction map, not inferences from the
scalar comparison.
   Finally differentiate the labelled identities before summing histories. A g mark either stays on the
distinguished input or hits one of finitely many local atoms, the scalar orbit, the extraction projector,
or a new periodic connector. In the first case the same pairing proves the same-order coefficient in the
statement. In every other case the marked child row and the trajectory ledger give the polynomial factor,
while the periodic hull or charged event remains present. This proves (196) through two marks.

5.5    Finite triangular source transport
Lemma 5.21 (Finite graded curvature-jet quotient). Fix an engineering cutoff D. Modulo vacuum
constants, total lattice differences, gauge and Bianchi identities, equation-of-motion/redundant directions,
and the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills coordinate, the local gauge-invariant curvature jets of
dimension at most D form a finite graded space
                                                      M
                                               J≤D =       J∆ .
                                                         ∆≤D

The same remains true after adjoining any fixed finite collection of retained, source, displacement, profile,
stress, rotation-defect, and one/two-root marks.
Proof. After a boundary-rooted tree gauge, one enlarged block cell contains a finite alphabet of Lie-algebra
variables and difference labels. Jets of bounded engineering dimension form a finite polynomial span.
Every listed quotient relation is linear on that span, and adjoining finitely many marks gives a finite
direct sum.
Lemma 5.22 (No reflection-even scalar of engineering dimension five). In four-dimensional pure fixed-G
lattice gauge theory, the signed-hypercubic and reflection-even gauge-invariant scalar jet quotient has no
engineering-dimension-five component.
Proof. A nonvacuum dimension-five scalar would have to contain two curvature factors and one covariant
difference, hence five coordinate indices before contraction. The invariant tensors of the signed hypercubic
group are generated by even-rank Kronecker contractions and the rank-four alternating tensor. They
cannot contract an odd total of five indices to a scalar without a preferred vector. Total differences and
Bianchi descendants are already removed in the quotient. Thus the reflection-even scalar sector jumps
from the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills density to dimension at least six.

Lemma 5.23 (No reflection-even rank-two curvature jet of dimension five). In the local gauge-invariant
rank-two curvature-jet quotient relevant to the stress/displacement source, the reflection-even engineering-
dimension-five component is zero.
Proof. A dimension-five curvature bilinear has the schematic Lorentz-index content F DF , hence five
tensor indices before contractions. To leave exactly two free rank-two indices one would have to contract
an odd set of three indices. The signed-hypercubic invariant tensors available in the reflection-even
sector are products of Kronecker deltas and pairs of Levi–Civita tensors, all of even rank. A single
Levi–Civita tensor is reflection odd. Thus no reflection-even constant coefficient tensor can perform the
required contraction. Terms in which the derivative is moved onto the whole bilinear are total differences,
while Bianchi and equation-of-motion descendants are already quotiented. The next possible rank-two
complement after dimension four therefore begins at dimension six.

Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time
quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form
under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor-
mations are changes of variables or observable identities, not replacement probability measures, and all
regular/irregular and half-cylinder labels are recombined before the OS form is taken. Therefore reflection
positivity passes to the common limiting family.
Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time
observables. Pull each Fi back through the exact block, fiber, and source identities to a gauge-invariant
function of the original positive-time links. Exact recombination makes this pullback independent of the
auxiliary labels. Reflection positivity of the Wilson measure gives
                                           X

                                           i,j

The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization
factors remain inside the same normalized integral once. Every entry of the finite matrix converges along
the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This
proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

8.2    Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
     even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
     tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
      presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar , and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

 Yang–Mills Existence, Mass Gap, and Nontriviality
for Fixed Compact Simple Gauge Groups via Karcher
      Blocking and Exact Haar–Pivot Matching
                                             Harold D. Kirk III1,2,∗
                         1
                             University of Memphis, Memphis, Tennessee 38152, USA
                                        2
                                            ORCID: 0009-0000-6014-8451

                                                August 3, 2026


                                                     Abstract
        Let G be a fixed compact connected simple Lie group and let ρ be a fixed faithful finite-dimensional
    unitary representation. We construct four-dimensional pure G Yang–Mills theory as a zero-spacing,
    infinite-volume limit of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures. The Wilson plaquette function
    is normalized at the identity to the background quadratic form used to define the coupling u = g 2 .
    A weighted forty-nine-path Karcher block, complete sixteen-alias normal section, charged chart-
    complement partition, and permanent rooted domain produce a uniform scale-two map on a centered
    invariant graph,
                                                 11 CA (G) log 2
                               u(2L) = u(L) +                    u(L)2 + OG (u(L)3 ).
                                                     24π 2

    The complete linear block certificate is independent of color and tensors with Ig ; all nonlinear
    constants are finite and may depend on G. At the physical handoff, a group-dependent product
    Sobolev order 2sG > dG /2 gives a Peter–Weyl Fourier–Wiener landing for the exact one-pivot and
    multipivot densities. Separated Haar pivots contract nonvacuum compact flux, optical covariance
    contracts normal-field stretches, and all remaining vertices retain their support charge. A single
    marked replica–BKAR forest then gives a boundary-to-center two-root connector and one physical
    clustering rate. General invariant-jet transport, displacement and stress Ward identities, finite
    dimension-four mixing, and a dimension-six rotation regulator yield a tempered, reflection-positive,
    translation-continuous, O(4)-covariant Schwinger family. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction gives a
    positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with unique vacuum and a strictly positive Hamiltonian
    gap. Endpoint-safe curvature-source transport produces a nonzero local vector with positive spectral
    mass in a finite interval above the gap. The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet
    observable theory. No estimate is asserted uniformly over the class of compact simple groups.

1     The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary
1.1    Problem and result
The constructive Yang–Mills problem asks for a nontrivial quantum gauge field theory on R4 satisfying
the Osterwalder–Schrader and Wightman requirements and possessing a strictly positive mass gap [1].
The physical scale, thermodynamic limit, zero lattice spacing, Euclidean covariance, local spectral content,
and non-abelian field geometry must be controlled in one compatible construction.
   Fix throughout a compact connected simple Lie group G, a faithful unitary representation ρ : G → U (Vρ )
of dimension dρ , and a positive normalization of the invariant quadratic form for which the adjoint color
factor is denoted CA (G). The Wilson plaquette function is
                                                        1
                                                                    
                                   wG,ρ (U ) = cρ 1 −     Re Tr ρ(U ) ,
                                                       dρ
where cρ > 0 is chosen once so that the Hessian at the identity is the quadratic form used by the
background-field coupling coordinate u = g 2 . This choice is a finite linear normalization of the bare
Wilson coupling and is part of the fixed-group input register.
Theorem 1.1 (Four-dimensional Yang–Mills construction for fixed compact simple G). For every fixed
pair (G, ρ) as above, there is a directed sequence of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures, with
microscopic spacing tending to zero and periodic volume tending to infinity, whose local gauge-invariant
curvature-jet Schwinger functions converge to a nontrivial Euclidean field theory on R4 .
   The limiting Schwinger family is tempered, permutation symmetric, reflection positive, translation
continuous, exponentially clustering, and O(4) covariant. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction yields a
positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with a unique vacuum and a number m⋆ (G) > 0 such that
                                              H Ω⊥ ≥ m⋆ (G).
On the centered invariant weak trajectory, the exact scale-two coupling map is
                                            11 CA (G) log 2
                           u(2L) = u(L) +                   u(L)2 + OG (u(L)3 ),
                                                 24π 2
with uniform volume and cutoff control in the permanent weak regime. The reconstructed theory contains
a nonzero reflected local curvature vector and positive spectral mass in a finite interval [m⋆ (G), M⋆ (G)].
1.2    Fixed-group conventions and finite register
Write g = Lie(G) and dG = dim G. All Haar measures are normalized. The finite-volume approximants
use the ordinary periodic link space GE(Λ) with periodic gauge transformations; no twisted boundary
condition or nontrivial finite-volume bundle sector is summed. This is sufficient for the claimed local
theory on the contractible space R4 . The bi-invariant metric, exponential coordinates, Laplacian ∆G ,
Casimir eigenvalues, and operator norms are those determined by the declared quadratic form. In
curvature formulas, Tr(XY ) denotes this normalized invariant bilinear pairing; raw representation traces
are written Tr ρ(·). Let                     
                                              dG                    dG
                                      sG :=       + 1,      2sG >       .
                                               4                      2
The fixed-group constant register CG consists of the finite quantities used below: the injectivity and
convexity radii, a positive local Karcher radius, finite holomorphic BCH and logarithm bounds through
order 2sG + 3, the faithful-action Hessian and complement charge, the Peter–Weyl constant, the finite
ordinary-periodic toron/seam template register, the adjoint color factor, and the finite invariant-jet
dimensions through the retained degree. Every statement called uniform is uniform in cutoff, volume,
directed depth, periodic wrapping, admitted exterior histories, and the finite reflected template family,
but its constants may depend on CG .
Proposition 1.2 (Reflection-positive Wilson input). For every finite periodic lattice Λ and u > 0, in the
ordinary periodic link sector,
                                                "                    #

is gauge invariant and reflection positive for coordinate reflections. Moreover wG,ρ ≥ 0, its zero set is the

                                     cG ∥X∥2 ≤ wG,ρ (eX ) ≤ CG ∥X∥2 .

Proof. The normalized character satisfies Re Tr ρ(U ) ≤ dρ , with equality only when ρ(U ) = I; faithfulness
gives U = e. The Hessian statement follows from the chosen normalization and compactness. Up to a field-
independent constant, each plaquette factor is exp{t(χρ +χρ )} with t > 0. Its power series is a nonnegative
linear combination of characters of tensor products of ρ and ρ̄, and their irreducible decompositions
have nonnegative integer multiplicities. The standard character-expansion reflection-positivity argument
therefore applies.

1.3     Proof architecture
The proof has four stages. First, the exact Karcher block is placed in a complete full-alias normal chart
and combined with a charged chart-complement partition, permanent analytic domain, marked connected
logarithm, Wick-stable jet block, and centered invariant graph. The folded spacetime symbol tensors
with Ig , while all nonlinear estimates are admitted by the finite register CG .
   Second, exact background-scheme conjugacy identifies the universal marginal coefficient      and a directed
schedule reaches the compact matching scale. The higher product Sobolev operator p (1 − ∆p )sG replaces
                                                                                       Q
the rank-one order and supplies the fixed-G Fourier–Wiener rows.
   Third, complete Haar and optical variables are kept in one partition function. A mixed replica–
BKAR forest makes every root-to-root path pay separated Haar contraction, optical covariance decay,
or pre-existing support charge. Ordinary-periodic toron backgrounds, seams, reflections, and wrapping
certificates enter only through the finite template theorem of Appendix D.
   Finally, a least-label source forest transports the complete invariant-jet system. The degree-four block
is group-independent because a compact simple Lie algebra has a unique invariant symmetric bilinear
form; possible cubic color tensors first enter the finite dimension-six block. Ward identities, rotation-defect
estimates, reflection-positive recombination, and Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction give covariance,
the unique vacuum, the gap, and noncollapse.

1.5     Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction
The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful
independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is
stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives
a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which
tensor with Ig , and which require new group data.
1.6     Scope
The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact
connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit,
control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum
limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link
group. The construction uses only the ordinary periodic finite-volume sector and retains all flat toron
backgrounds occurring in that sector; no claim about a sum over twisted boundary conditions is made or
needed.

2     Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking
Let Λ be a finite periodic four-dimensional lattice and use the reflection-positive Wilson measure of
Proposition 1.2. For each oriented retained link, use the straight two-link path with weight 7/8 and the
forty-eight signed-corner staples with weight 1/384 each. On a fixed strongly convex identity tube, define
the retained link as their weighted Karcher mean in the bi-invariant metric.
Lemma 2.1 (Fixed-group convex Karcher and holomorphic atlas). There are radii 0 < r0,G < r1,G
such that the squared-distance functions, product, inverse, exponential, logarithm, and weighted Karcher
critical point extend holomorphically to the corresponding local complexification tube. The Karcher Hessian
is bounded below there by a positive constant depending only on G. All derivatives through arbitrary
prescribed finite order are bounded on the smaller tube.
Proof. A compact Lie group with a bi-invariant analytic metric has positive injectivity and strong-convexity
radii. The sectional curvature is bounded because G is compact, and the Hessian comparison theorem
gives a positive lower Hessian bound on a sufficiently small ball. In a faithful matrix representation
the local complexification is an open neighborhood in the complexified group; multiplication, inversion,
exponential, and the principal logarithm are holomorphic there. The analytic implicit-function theorem
applied to the Karcher critical equation gives the holomorphic mean. Cauchy estimates on nested compact
tubes give every fixed derivative order; standard compact Lie-group analytic facts are summarized in
[15].
Proposition 2.2 (Exact block covariance and local submersion). The retained map is gauge covariant,
orientation covariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and aligned-reflection covariant. Its linearization at
the flat connection has full retained rank. On one fixed weak tube it admits a finite-volume analytic
normal section whose matrix kernel and first three marked derivatives are exponentially local, uniformly
in volume.
Proof. Every path has the same endpoints, and the weighted squared-distance functional is invariant
under simultaneous left and right multiplication. Uniqueness of the Karcher minimizer gives gauge and
orientation covariance. The signed-corner path family is invariant under the lattice symmetry group.
At the flat point, the exact Fourier symbol is the color-free spacetime symbol tensored with Ig and has
acoustic value 2I ⊗ Ig and a uniformly gapped normal frame. The full-alias section and normal-fiber
contraction are proved in the later normal-chart chapters.
Proposition 2.3 (Exact coarea–fiber identity). At finite volume, in the normal fiber chart,

                                    dU = J0 det(I + DN P )−1 dW dζ,

where the determinant is the ordinary finite-dimensional determinant. The fixed linear determinant is
owned once as a scalar normalization, and the field-dependent logarithm is one connected analytic activity.

forests: after one mixed contraction every outer quotient is loop-free, disjoint mixed parts are impossible,
and a forest with no mixed member leaves one marked loop. Lemma 5.9 supplies its degree-six kernel;
the unused support exponent absorbs the finite edge-pair and localization polynomials.

marked component of engineering degree d contributes to P⋆ with an explicit ad−4 and an annulus row
bounded by C2(d−4)j . If d > 4, the annulus sum is geometric:
                                                   J(a)
                                                   X
                                         a   d−4
                                                          2(d−4)j = O(1),
                                                   j=0

so it produces a finite local scheme row and no logarithm. A logarithm occurs only at the marginal
exponent d − 4 = 0. Higher external-momentum Taylor coefficients can lower the annulus exponent to
zero, but then they retain the explicit ad−4 ; for d ≥ 6 their logarithmic row is O(a2 | log a|). Hence an
unsuppressed P⋆ log a term would require a nonzero marked degree-four input jet, which is absent. No
assertion that a redundant dimension-four insertion is annihilated support by support is used: such rows
have already been extracted and are not present in HX .
   Finite-support multiplicity is polynomial in diam X; for a diam X > 1, the strict support reserve
dominates every prescribed power of a.

                           (2)
                         |IX (a)| ≤ C(1 + diam X)q e−(a0 +η)τ (X) (1 + a2 | log a|).
The unsuppressed physical logarithm vanishes support by support:
                                                   (2)
                                     [log a] P⋆ IX = 0        for every X.                                (25)
Thus the full centered invariant family differs from the canonically normalized Wilson family by one finite,
scale-independent C 2 scheme map through the two consumed background marks.
Proof. The support polynomial in Lemma 5.13 is absorbed by the strict exponent reserve. The finite-
support amplitudes and their two background derivatives may therefore be summed before removing the
cutoff. At the quadratic order in the marginal coordinate needed for the one-step coefficient, apply the
complete SI–6B/SI–7M lattice BPHZL packet, the exhaustive ledger of Lemma 5.10, and the complete
post-contraction admission check of Lemma 5.12 to every mixed Wilson/stable term. A marginal proper
subgraph containing only Wilson vertices is exactly a standard background-field subgraph and is subtracted
in the SI–6 scheme. After all such subgraphs are contracted, every remaining quotient graph contains at
least one stable marked insertion and hence has positive irrelevant excess, at least two in the reflection-even
physical sector. Its overall annulus sum is therefore a power row or an explicitly a2 | log a|-suppressed
row, not an unsuppressed logarithm. Multiple stable insertions only increase that excess. The marked
stable-fiber identity removes every degree-four input row before this forest analysis. Uniform convergence
leaves one finite C 2 local scheme term and no new unsuppressed logarithmic coefficient.
Lemma 5.15 (Scale-independent finite scheme map). Let gW be the local standard coupling on the
canonically rescaled pure Wilson family and let g⋆ be the same local functional on the full centered
invariant family. On the common weak interval there is one C 2 map
                                  s(g) = g + cs g 2 + O(g 3 ),      s′ (0) = 1,
independent of the dyadic lattice spacing, such that
                                                    g⋆ = s(gW )
at both ends of every factor-two step. Consequently
                                            Σstd = s ◦ ΣW ◦ s−1 .
The C 3 -flat irregular complement is included in s; no analyticity at g = 0 is claimed or needed.
Proof. Evaluate the same canonically rescaled nonexceptional projector P⋆ on the two full weak families.
Their tree Hessians coincide. Their first difference is the invariant-graph tangent uH1 , whose vacuum
scalar and complete dimension-four retained projection vanish. Lemma 5.14 proves, rather than assumes,
that the entire exponentially quasi-local insertion family contributes a finite scale-independent transverse
scheme row and no new unsuppressed log a coefficient. SI–6 excludes an independent background
dimension-four counterterm. The inner-chart difference is therefore a finite C 2 unit-tangent redefinition.
U4 adds a C 3 -flat term through the two consumed background marks, which changes neither s′ (0) nor its
quadratic coefficient. Canonical rescaling identifies the profile, gauge fermion, subtraction projector, and
cutoff with the same unit-lattice template at every dyadic scale, so the same map s occurs before and
after the step. The displayed conjugacy follows from the definitions.

5.3    Domain compactness and chart invariance
Let Ba,N be the rooted activity space with norm
                                               X
                                 ∥F ∥a,N = sup   ea|X| ∥FX ∥C N (KX ) ,
                                                  e
                                                      X∋e

where each KX is a compact finite-dimensional product chart and, for every m, only finitely many rooted
connected support shapes of size at most m occur.
Theorem 5.18 (Compact weakening). If a > 0, a′ > 0, and a′ < a, every bounded subset of Ba,N ,
N ≥ 1, is relatively compact in Ba′ ,N −1 .
Proof. Let F be bounded by M in Ba,N . For a cutoff L,
                                 X      ′                        ′
                            sup       ea |X| ∥FX ∥C N −1 ≤ e−(a−a )L M,
                                  e
                                        X∋e
                                       |X|>L

uniformly in F ∈ F.
  For |X| ≤ L, only finitely many rooted support shapes occur. On each shape, the C N (KX ) bound is
uniform, and the compact embedding
                                           C N (KX ) ⋐ C N −1 (KX )
follows from Arzela–Ascoli in finitely many coordinate charts. A diagonal subsequence converges on
all shapes of size at most L. Letting L increase and using the uniform tail estimate proves relative
compactness in Ba′ ,N −1 .
Theorem 5.19 (Exact fiber-chart invariance). Let Ψ1 (w, z1 ) and Ψ2 (w, z2 ) be two exact C 1 fiber charts
for the same finite-dimensional submersion B(U ) = w. Suppose their images agree on the integration
region and their transition map is orientation preserving. Then, for every integrable density F ,
                      Z                                   Z
                         F (Ψ1 (w, z1 )) J1 (w, z1 ) dz1 = F (Ψ2 (w, z2 )) J2 (w, z2 ) dz2 .

Consequently the normalized conditional expectation, its connected logarithm, and every background
derivative of the complete local standard-scheme coordinate and its quadratic coefficient are independent
of the chosen exact fiber chart.


                                                        48
Proof. The first identity is the ordinary change-of-variables theorem on the fiber. Division by the same
fiber mass gives equality of normalized conditional expectations. Connected logarithms are functions
of those expectations. Differentiation is permitted on any common dominated marked domain. The
determinant statement follows because the two Jacobians are related by the determinant of the transition
map, which cancels under the change of variables. It must not be inserted a second time.

5.5    Finite triangular source transport
Lemma 5.21 (Finite graded curvature-jet quotient). Fix an engineering cutoff D. Modulo vacuum
constants, total lattice differences, gauge and Bianchi identities, equation-of-motion/redundant directions,
and the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills coordinate, the local gauge-invariant curvature jets of
dimension at most D form a finite graded space
                                                      M
                                               J≤D =       J∆ .
                                                         ∆≤D

The same remains true after adjoining any fixed finite collection of retained, source, displacement, profile,
stress, rotation-defect, and one/two-root marks.
Proof. After a boundary-rooted tree gauge, one enlarged block cell contains a finite alphabet of Lie-algebra
variables and difference labels. Jets of bounded engineering dimension form a finite polynomial span.
Every listed quotient relation is linear on that span, and adjoining finitely many marks gives a finite
direct sum.
Lemma 5.22 (No reflection-even scalar of engineering dimension five). In four-dimensional pure fixed-G
lattice gauge theory, the signed-hypercubic and reflection-even gauge-invariant scalar jet quotient has no
engineering-dimension-five component.
Proof. A nonvacuum dimension-five scalar would have to contain two curvature factors and one covariant
difference, hence five coordinate indices before contraction. The invariant tensors of the signed hypercubic
group are generated by even-rank Kronecker contractions and the rank-four alternating tensor. They
cannot contract an odd total of five indices to a scalar without a preferred vector. Total differences and
Bianchi descendants are already removed in the quotient. Thus the reflection-even scalar sector jumps
from the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills density to dimension at least six.

Lemma 5.23 (No reflection-even rank-two curvature jet of dimension five). In the local gauge-invariant
rank-two curvature-jet quotient relevant to the stress/displacement source, the reflection-even engineering-
dimension-five component is zero.
Proof. A dimension-five curvature bilinear has the schematic Lorentz-index content F DF , hence five
tensor indices before contractions. To leave exactly two free rank-two indices one would have to contract
an odd set of three indices. The signed-hypercubic invariant tensors available in the reflection-even
sector are products of Kronecker deltas and pairs of Levi–Civita tensors, all of even rank. A single
Levi–Civita tensor is reflection odd. Thus no reflection-even constant coefficient tensor can perform the
required contraction. Terms in which the derivative is moved onto the whole bilinear are total differences,
while Bianchi and equation-of-motion descendants are already quotiented. The next possible rank-two
complement after dimension four therefore begins at dimension six.

 the signed hypercubic
group are generated by even-rank Kronecker contractions and the rank-four alternating tensor. They
cannot contract an odd total of five indices to a scalar without a preferred vector. Total differences and
Bianchi descendants are already removed in the quotient. Thus the reflection-even scalar sector jumps
from the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills density to dimension at least six.

Lemma 5.23 (No reflection-even rank-two curvature jet of dimension five). In the local gauge-invariant
rank-two curvature-jet quotient relevant to the stress/displacement source, the reflection-even engineering-
dimension-five component is zero.
Proof. A dimension-five curvature bilinear has the schematic Lorentz-index content F DF , hence five
tensor indices before contractions. To leave exactly two free rank-two indices one would have to contract
an odd set of three indices. The signed-hypercubic invariant tensors available in the reflection-even
sector are products of Kronecker deltas and pairs of Levi–Civita tensors, all of even rank. A single
Levi–Civita tensor is reflection odd. Thus no reflection-even constant coefficient tensor can perform the
required contraction. Terms in which the derivative is moved onto the whole bilinear are total differences,
while Bianchi and equation-of-motion descendants are already quotiented. The next possible rank-two
complement after dimension four therefore begins at dimension six.

Lemma 8.22 (The active source space contains the full tensor block). The coefficient-labelled represen-

Proof. Lemma 5.21 allows arbitrary fixed tensor, profile, stress, and rotation-defect marks. Treating the

of J≤D . Signed-hypercubic symmetry constrains the matrix entries but does not quotient out source
labels. Hence the complete filtered transport retains the whole span used below.

Lemma 8.27 (Exact profile division). At every fixed field degree, the constant-profile vector kernel of the
complete improved local score through engineering dimension four vanishes at zero profile momentum. It
therefore admits exact finite Laurent division
                                               X
                                     Vµ (p) =   (eiapα − 1)Wµα (p).                                   (148)
                                                      α

Thus the apparent factor v/a in (124) is converted before absolute root summation into a bounded discrete
derivative of v.
Proof. A zero-profile-momentum coefficient would be a local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector
of engineering dimension at most four. No such vector exists: a connection mass term is not gauge
invariant; Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 because g is semisimple; traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of the same
zero trace; and two curvature two-forms cannot be contracted with invariant metric tensors while leaving
exactly one free vector index. The epsilon tensor has the wrong reflection parity. Hence the finite Laurent
polynomial Vµ (z) vanishes at z1 = · · · = z4 = 1. Successive telescoping in the variables zα writes it in the
ideal generated by zα − 1 and preserves finite range, proving (148).

Corollary 8.29 (Finite-cutoff Ward identity with vanishing geometric defect). For every bounded
geometric source Fa and compactly supported smooth profile v,

                                        Ea [δvgeo Fa ] = Ea [Fa Ta (v)] + O(a2 ),                      (152)

with the same statement after passage through the exact projective conditional system. Along the common
continuum subsequence, the error vanishes and the local translation Ward identity is represented by the
projective limits of Tj0 and Θj .

Proof. Apply finite-dimensional Haar integration by parts to X cl and use Proposition 8.21 on the left-hand
side. Theorem 8.28 supplies uniform integrability and projective transport on the right.

Finite incidence identities The finite clover and incidence formulas satisfy:
1. exact cancellation of the distinguished-link derivative in the two-plaquette clover;
2. exact gauge covariance under arbitrary lattice G gauge transformations;
3. midpoint/reflection cancellation showing an O(a3 ) per-link strip defect;

Lemma 8.27 (Exact profile division). At every fixed field degree, the constant-profile vector kernel of the
complete improved local score through engineering dimension four vanishes at zero profile momentum. It
therefore admits exact finite Laurent division
                                               X
                                     Vµ (p) =   (eiapα − 1)Wµα (p).                                   (148)
                                                      α

Thus the apparent factor v/a in (124) is converted before absolute root summation into a bounded discrete
derivative of v.
Proof. A zero-profile-momentum coefficient would be a local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector
of engineering dimension at most four. No such vector exists: a connection mass term is not gauge
invariant; Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 because g is semisimple; traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of the same
zero trace; and two curvature two-forms cannot be contracted with invariant metric tensors while leaving
exactly one free vector index. The epsilon tensor has the wrong reflection parity. Hence the finite Laurent
polynomial Vµ (z) vanishes at z1 = · · · = z4 = 1. Successive telescoping in the variables zα writes it in the
ideal generated by zα − 1 and preserves finite range, proving (148).

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines

finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its
distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

Result and local-field realization
Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc-
tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger
algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).

action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )

derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path

finite source normalization.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

and every periodic wrapping certificate. The acoustic toron/background variables remain explicit and are
never inverted. No constant uniform over different groups is asserted.
Proof. Lift a seam representative to the universal covering lattice. A flat background is gauged to the
identity there; deck translation in direction µ acts on Lie-algebra fields by the unitary operator Ad(hµ ).
Since the hµ commute, these unitary operators are simultaneously diagonalizable over the complexified
color space. On each joint color eigenspace the folded block and optical symbols are therefore the
identity-background, color-free symbols evaluated at a real quasi-momentum shifted by the corresponding
deck phase. The estimates
                                                  49                      8
                                Af (k)Af (k)∗ ⪰      I4 ,   ∥P0 (k)∥2 ≤      ,
                                                   2                      49
and the printed complex-strip and optical lower bounds hold for every real quasi-momentum. They
consequently survive every toron phase without a loss of constant. The retained acoustic variables,
including toron tangents, are kept in the exterior/background register; only the same normal optical
complement is inverted.
   Lemma D.2 supplies the nonlinear, coarea, Haar, and marked derivative bounds uniformly over a finite
toron/seam cover. On the covering lattice the local gauge trivialization sends every plaquette holonomy
to a conjugate of the corresponding fluctuation plaquette. The representation-normalized Wilson function
is conjugation invariant, so the charge and its differentiated finite-word ledger are unchanged by this
trivialization. The 2sG differentiated bad-core row of Theorem 3.20 is therefore uniform on the same
cover. Consequently the complete one-pivot score and Haar minorization retain their printed constants
after taking the finite template maximum. On intersections use the fixed least-label owner, so no factor is
duplicated. A wrapping certificate records only the four lattice directions, the fixed block range, a finite
seam crossing pattern, and an aligned reflection type; there are finitely many such local templates at one
normalized step. Their iteration is already controlled by the permanent wrapping ideal rather than by a
sum over topological sectors. Taking the finite maximum proves the stated admission and leaves the joint
Haar–optical boundary-to-center mechanism unchanged.

E     Invariant tensor spaces through engineering dimension six
The low-dimensional color input can be stated without choosing a basis. The degree-two invariant
polynomial is unique for every simple g. A primitive degree-three invariant occurs only for Ar , r ≥ 2.
Hence the reflection-even dimension-four scalar block is one-dimensional for every G, while the dimension-
six block may contain the additional cubic curvature contraction in type A. The antisymmetric structure


                                                      111
tensor is always present through the bracket but contributes only inside the already retained covariant
derivative and commutator jets.
   The manuscript uses the complete finite quotient generated by curvature, covariant derivatives, metric
and epsilon contractions, invariant color tensors, total-difference classes, and equation-of-motion contacts
through the declared engineering cutoff. No argument relies on a hand-picked SU(2) basis once this
quotient is used.

fixed-group input in the register CG , the first proof call at which that dependence enters, and the later
statements that consume it. In particular, the completion proof in Theorem 11.1 does not rely on an
unprinted constant or on a rank-one identity.

   The table also separates the common mechanisms into four logical classes. Replica–BKAR, Kotecký–
Preiss, history compatibility, support reporting, least-label source forests, OS reconstruction, and spectral-

5.5    Finite triangular source transport
Lemma 5.21 (Finite graded curvature-jet quotient). Fix an engineering cutoff D. Modulo vacuum
constants, total lattice differences, gauge and Bianchi identities, equation-of-motion/redundant directions,
and the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills coordinate, the local gauge-invariant curvature jets of
dimension at most D form a finite graded space
                                                      M
                                               J≤D =       J∆ .
                                                         ∆≤D

The same remains true after adjoining any fixed finite collection of retained, source, displacement, profile,
stress, rotation-defect, and one/two-root marks.
Proof. After a boundary-rooted tree gauge, one enlarged block cell contains a finite alphabet of Lie-algebra
variables and difference labels. Jets of bounded engineering dimension form a finite polynomial span.
Every listed quotient relation is linear on that span, and adjoining finitely many marks gives a finite
direct sum.
Lemma 5.22 (No reflection-even scalar of engineering dimension five). In four-dimensional pure fixed-G
lattice gauge theory, the signed-hypercubic and reflection-even gauge-invariant scalar jet quotient has no
engineering-dimension-five component.
Proof. A nonvacuum dimension-five scalar would have to contain two curvature factors and one covariant
difference, hence five coordinate indices before contraction. The invariant tensors of the signed hypercubic
group are generated by even-rank Kronecker contractions and the rank-four alternating tensor. They
cannot contract an odd total of five indices to a scalar without a preferred vector. Total differences and
Bianchi descendants are already removed in the quotient. Thus the reflection-even scalar sector jumps
from the extracted dimension-four Yang–Mills density to dimension at least six.

Lemma 5.23 (No reflection-even rank-two curvature jet of dimension five). In the local gauge-invariant
rank-two curvature-jet quotient relevant to the stress/displacement source, the reflection-even engineering-
dimension-five component is zero.
Proof. A dimension-five curvature bilinear has the schematic Lorentz-index content F DF , hence five
tensor indices before contractions. To leave exactly two free rank-two indices one would have to contract
an odd set of three indices. The signed-hypercubic invariant tensors available in the reflection-even
sector are products of Kronecker deltas and pairs of Levi–Civita tensors, all of even rank. A single
Levi–Civita tensor is reflection odd. Thus no reflection-even constant coefficient tensor can perform the
required contraction. Terms in which the derivative is moved onto the whole bilinear are total differences,
while Bianchi and equation-of-motion descendants are already quotiented. The next possible rank-two
complement after dimension four therefore begins at dimension six.

Theorem 5.26 (Weak-regime finite triangular source renormalization). For every fixed engineering cutoff
D, there are normalized source coordinates in the weak regime such that:
   (i) principal same-degree transport and its inverse grow by at most a fixed power of the running
       logarithm;
  (ii) the complementary scalar source block is strictly contracting;
 (iii) a dimension-∆ + 2 defect relative to a principal dimension-∆ source contributes

                                                     r2 L(r)pD

      at physical scale r;
 (iv) all fixed marked rows obey uniform finite Schwartz-seminorm bounds.
  This theorem stops at the compact first-crossing handoff. It does not invoke the later macrobridge or
matching transfer.
Proof. Normalize each principal block by the fundamental matrix in Lemma 5.25. The strict complemen-
tary contraction sums the triangular inhomogeneous forcing by a geometric series. Canonical dimensional
scaling supplies the factor r2 for a degree-∆ + 2 defect; conjugation by the principal frame contributes at
most L(r)pD . The finite marked fiber and the exact marked one-step bounds give the stated Schwartz
estimates.

Lemma 8.27 (Exact profile division). At every fixed field degree, the constant-profile vector kernel of the

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.

Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].

Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports
of ΘF and τt F are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F ) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give
                               Z ∞
                          0≤        e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F ], T (t)[F ]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt .                       (168)
                                  0

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.
